Magnetohydrodynamic drag on an oscillating
sphere in a rotating cavity
David C´ebron1†, and Paolo Personnettaz1,2
1Univ. Grenoble Alpes, Univ. Savoie Mont Blanc, CNRS, IRD, Univ. Gustave Eiffel, ISTerre, 38000
Grenoble, France
2CNES - Centre National d’Etudes Spatiales, 2 Place Maurice Quentin, 75039, Paris, France
(Received xx; revised xx; accepted xx)
We analyse the magnetohydrodynamic drag on a sphere undergoing small-amplitude trans-
lational oscillations in a rotating spherical cavity. This provides a canonical model for
oscillatory flows in confined rotating magnetohydrodynamic systems, where dissipation
arises from the poorly constrained coupling between magnetic fields, rotation and viscosity.
Such flows occur in planetary interiors, notably driven by the translational oscillations of
the Earth’s inner core along linear or circular trajectories (the polar and equatorial Slichter
modes). They may also arise in the thin subsurface oceans of icy moons where strong
confinement is expected. Previous theoretical studies considered only simplified limits,
restricted to the polar mode: Stokes (1851) solved the viscous bounded problem without
rotation or magnetic effects, revealing the importance of pressure, whereas Buffett & Goertz
(1995) examined magnetic tension in a non-rotating inviscid unbounded fluid, neglecting
magnetic pressure and confinement. We develop a unified boundary-layer framework for
magnetic and viscous effects in a fluid shell bounded by two solid regions with possibly
different electromagnetic properties. Large electromagnetic contrasts arise even in simple
laboratory configurations, such as an iron sphere oscillating in a liquid-metal (e.g. Galinstan).
We derive boundary layers and obtain the magnetohydrodynamic drag from Alfv´en-wave
radiation, viscous effects and Ohmic dissipation (accounting for pressure effects). The theory
is extended to non-axisymmetric equatorial modes and to rotation perturbations of the polar
mode, with stronger rotation treated through magnetohydrodynamic Stokes-Ekman boundary
layers and a corrected inviscid solution of Busse (1974). Our magnetohydrodynamic
simulations validate our theory, providing a quantitative framework for planetary interiors.

1. Introduction
1.1. Geophysical background
The Earth’s solid inner core can slightly translate within the liquid outer core relative to
the planetary centre. Such motions may arise when seismic or asteroid impact energies
are comparable to the gravitational energy of the interior (Rosat 2011). A force balance
shows that the inner-core centre of mass behaves as a damped harmonic oscillator, where the
Archimedean force is the dominant restoring term. Earth’s rotation splits these oscillations
into one polar and two equatorial Slichter modes (Slichter 1961), the latter being an orbital-
like motion in the equatorial plane. Their dynamics remain less well constrained than
rotational modes driven by external interactions (e.g. Le Bars et al. 2015).
The oscillation period follows from Newton’s second law applied to the inner core,
including the Archimedean restoring force and the fluid added mass (Slichter 1961).
Rotational, inertial and viscous effects were examined by Won & Kuo (1973). Moving
beyond lumped models, Busse (1974), noted B74 hereafter, solved the Euler equations in
a rotating cavity, showing that confinement and fluid Coriolis effects nearly compensate,
yielding only small corrections to previous period estimates; he also derived a closed-form
inviscid velocity field for polar oscillations. The linear hydrodynamic problem was later
investigated numerically by Rieutord (2002), who formulated a coupled eigenvalue problem
for a slightly oscillating inner core in a rotating, isentropic and viscous spherical fluid shell.
Viscous and Ohmic dissipation have received less attention. Neglecting magnetic-pressure
effects, Buffett & Goertz (1995) used a boundary-layer model to derive the magnetic-tension
contribution to the Lorentz force for polar oscillations in an inviscid, non-rotating and
unbounded fluid. Smylie & McMillan (1998) analysed the three modes in a non-magnetic,
bounded, viscous and rotating fluid, constructing the basic flow from products of Legendre
functions (inertial-mode approaches of Greenspan 1968) and including Stokes-Ekman
boundary layers. They obtained the inviscid pressure force and viscous tangential stresses,
but neglected viscous corrections to the pressure required to recover the exact solution of
Stokes (1851). This formulation was used by Smylie (1999) to infer an unrealistically large
viscosity near the inner core (see also Smylie & McMillan 2000). Despite these efforts, the
relative importance of dissipation mechanisms remains uncertain (Rosat 2011).
After more than two decades of limited attention, the dissipation of Earth’s core Slichter
modes was revisited by Personnettaz et al. (2026). Combining magnetohydrodynamic (MHD)
simulations with scaling arguments, they estimated both Ohmic and viscous dissipation and
inferred quality factors and decay times for Earth’s core. Beyond its geophysical motivation,
however, the problem is attractive for its simple geometry, which is amenable to analytical
approaches such as potential-flow theory and boundary-layer analysis. In the following, we
review the relevant contributions from both fluid-dynamical and MHD perspectives.
1.2. Fluid dynamics context
The flow and forces generated by an oscillating sphere form a canonical problem in fluid
mechanics, relevant from laboratory experiments to planetary interiors. Classical solutions
describe viscous effects in simple configurations, whereas many geophysical systems addi-
tionally involve rotation, confinement and magnetic fields. In such environments, oscillations
of electrically conducting fluids generate coupled viscous, rotational and magnetic boundary
layers, which have not yet been addressed within a unified framework.
The drag on a sphere moving in a fluid has been studied for more than two centuries.
Early work was motivated by pendulum experiments aimed at determining the Earth’s figure,
requiring accurate estimates of fluid resistance (Darrigol 2002). In this context, motion is
typically along a straight line (polar-mode forcing), and, unless stated otherwise, the studies

below refer to this configuration. Fluid resistance was first measured by Buat (1779) and
Bessel (1828) and initially attributed to inertial effects. Poisson (1831) then proposed a
mathematical solution, partly consistent with added-mass estimates. However, controlled gas
experiments by Sabine (1829) demonstrated that viscosity, not only density, influences the
oscillation period, challenging the view that viscosity was exclusive to liquids. This led to
Stokes’ seminal contributions. In Art. 9 of Stokes (1843), the inviscid flow generated by an
oscillating sphere in a concentric spherical envelope was derived, correcting Poisson’s result.
Viscous effects were later incorporated in Stokes (1851), yielding both the flow and forces
and providing one of the earliest successful applications of the Navier-Stokes equations with
boundary conditions (Darrigol 2002). These results were confirmed experimentally by Meyer
(1871) and extended by Basset (1888) to arbitrary linear acceleration.
Extensions to non-spherical geometries followed. Using the potential theory of Green
(1828), Kirchhoff (1876) derived solutions for irrotational flows around ellipsoids (e.g.
pp. 141, 144 of Lamb 1924, for prolate and oblate cases), while Oberbeck (1876) treated
viscous flows. Spherical confinement for prolate spheroids was approximated by B74. A
general overview is provided by Happel & Brenner (1960), with analytical expressions for
the drag on oscillating spheroids given by Lai & Mockros (1972).
The influence of rotation on the flow generated by a moving sphere was first examined by
Taylor (1922) and G¨ortler (1944), focusing on oscillations with frequency smaller than twice
the rotation rate. In this regime, Stewartson (1953) obtained the drag in an unbounded inviscid
fluid, where inertial wave radiation produces a wave-drag force (Mason 1975, 1977). In
contrast, planetary oscillations typically occur at frequencies large compared with the rotation
rate. Treating rotation as a perturbation, Singh (1964) derived the flow for polar oscillations in
an unbounded fluid, showing that rotation induces an azimuthal component without altering
the leading-order force. Approximating the effects of a spherical confinement, B74 obtained
the pressure drag in a rotating inviscid spherical cavity.
Beyond linear responses, oscillatory motion can generate steady streaming through
nonlinear advection (Riley 2001), leading to mean zonal flows in rotating systems (C´ebron
et al. 2021). This second-order effect was analysed for oscillating spheres by Wang (1965) and
Riley (1966) in the high-Strouhal-number regime, defined by a small oscillation amplitude
relative to the sphere radius. Extensions to oblate spheroids were obtained by Rednikov
& Sadhal (2004), and experimental visualisations by Kotas et al. (2007) confirmed the
resulting streaming flows. More recently, Kozlov et al. (2017); Subbotin & Shiryaeva (2021)
reproduced equatorial oscillations of a solid sphere within a rotating spherical shell. These

et al. 2020). However, the oscillation amplitudes in these experiments exceed the viscous
boundary-layer thickness, placing them outside the regime of interest of this study.
Magnetic effects on a steadily moving sphere in a conducting fluid were first studied in
the late 1950s (Stewartson 1956; Chester 1957; Reitz & Foldy 1961), and remain of current
interest (Delacroix & Davoust 2018; Liu et al. 2025). Singh (1965) extended the oscillatory
problem to conducting fluids in the presence of a magnetic field, demonstrating the existence
of Alfv´en waves. This work was further developed by Motz (1966a,b), who investigated
experimentally the drag in strong-field regimes using a dielectric sphere oscillating in mercury
under an axial magnetic field. In these experiments, the magnetic skin depth exceeded the
gap width, preventing the formation of magnetic boundary layers. A summary of the main
theoretical contributions and their assumptions is provided in table 1.
1.3. Motivations of this work
This work provides the theoretical basis for the scaling laws of Personnettaz et al. (2026).
The analysis extends beyond Earth’s configuration (a deep outer core with radii ratio 0.35)
to arbitrary layer geometries and distinct electrical properties in each domain. The problem
reduces to a solid, possibly conducting sphere undergoing rapid, small-amplitude oscillations
within a rotating, viscous, electrically conducting fluid-filled spherical shell bounded by a
solid domain and subject to an imposed magnetic field. The oscillation amplitude 𝜖𝑠is
assumed to be the smallest spatial scale, smaller than both the inner-sphere radius and
the gap width, corresponding to the high-Strouhal-number regime (Wang 1965) relevant to
planetary interiors, with typical amplitudes 1–5 mm (Rosat 2011; Coyette & Hoolst 2014).
Planetary liquid layers span depths ranging from shallow subsurface oceans (kilometres) to
deep molten cores (thousands of kilometres). Bounding effects are conveniently characterised
by the inner-to-outer radius ratio 𝑎(figure 1a), whose wide range leads to diverse confinement
regimes, particularly pronounced in Mercury’s core and icy-moon oceans, motivating
extensions beyond unbounded models (e.g. Buffett & Goertz 1995, BG95 hereafter). The
viscous and magnetic skin depths, based on their respective diffusivities and oscillation or

Magnetic fields in planetary bodies originate from internal dynamos (e.g. Earth’s core) or
external forcing (e.g. Jupiter’s field on Europa) and often display complex spatial and temporal
variability. On the short oscillation timescales considered here (hours), the imposed field is
taken steady. Its strength is characterised by the Lundquist number 𝐿𝑢𝜔= 𝑉𝐴𝐿𝜂/(

𝜔, with Λ < 1 corresponding to diffusive behaviour (magnetic skin layer) and Λ > 1
to Alfv´en-wave emission. Most planets and moons lie in the diffusive regime (figure 1b),
especially subsurface oceans with low conductivity (≈1 S/m) and weak external forcing,
while Earth lies near the transition (Personnettaz et al. 2026). Electrical properties may differ
between fluid and solid domains: metallic cores are comparably conducting in both phases,
outer solid regions may be insulating or conducting, and subsurface-ocean boundaries are
typically insulating. Arbitrary properties in each domain is therefore considered in this work.
Six ingredients govern the problem: the oscillation type (polar or equatorial), confinement
(bounded or unbounded), fluid viscosity (inviscid or viscous), rotation, electrical properties of
the three domains (inner solid sphere, fluid shell, outer solid shell), and the imposed magnetic
field. As reviewed below, no previous study incorporates all these effects simultaneously. We
therefore examine their coupled influence on the drag force, filling this gap.
Without magnetic field, the viscous force on a sphere arises from both pressure and
viscous stresses. For stress-free and no-slip spheres, Moore (1959) and Smylie & McMillan
(1998, 2000) retained only viscous stresses (normal and tangential, respectively), recovering
two-thirds of the total force obtained by Levich (1949) and Stokes (1851). As shown by
Kang & Leal (1988) for stress-free spheres, the missing contribution originates from the
viscous pressure correction induced by boundary-layer radial flow, required to recover the
total force. This correction can equivalently be obtained using the ’power approach’ (§5.13 of
Batchelor 1967), as discussed by Joseph & Wang (2004). Building on these results, we extend
Stokes (1851) and Smylie & McMillan (1998) by including rotational effects and viscously
modified pressure contributions, yielding a consistent expression for the viscous drag on a
no-slip sphere oscillating in a bounded rotating fluid. The MHD boundary-layer approach of
BG95—restricted to polar forcing in an inviscid, non-rotating, unbounded fluid—is further
generalised to compute MHD drag for both polar and equatorial oscillations, including
viscous, confinement, and rotational effects. The formulation is finally extended to arbitrary
conductivity and permeability contrasts across solid interfaces.

1.4. Structure of the work
The paper is organised as follows. The problem is formulated in §2. The theoretical and
numerical methods used to integrate the equations are described in §3. The non-magnetic
bulk flows driven by the oscillation, together with their rotational corrections and associated
viscous dissipation, are presented in §4. Magnetohydrodynamic boundary layers, drag, and
dissipation are analysed in §5 and §6 for inviscid and viscous fluids, respectively. Finally, §7
discusses the results and their implications.
2. Description of the problem
In this section, we derive the governing equations within the continuum mechanics frame-
work. Next, we explicitly characterise the forces exerted on the inner solid sphere, concluding
with a concise discussion of the dissipation mechanisms and their mathematical formulation.
2.1. Governing equations

magnetic diffusivity 𝜂𝑓= (𝜇𝑓𝜎𝑓)−1. The spherical boundaries separate the fluid from inner

Neglecting magnetic and rotational effects, the oscillating no-slip sphere was analysed
by Stokes (1851), who showed that 𝑭𝑛+ 𝑭𝑣is the sum of a viscous drag proportional
to 𝑽𝑠(vanishing as 𝜈→0) and an inertial contribution proportional to d𝑡𝑽𝑠, written
−𝐶𝑎𝑚𝑓d𝑡𝑽𝑠, where 𝐶𝑎→𝐶𝑖
𝑎≠0 in the inviscid limit owing to 𝑭𝑝. Although the steady
result 𝑭𝑛+ 𝑭𝑣= −6𝜋𝜌𝑓𝜈𝑎𝑠𝑽𝑠for 𝜔𝑠→0 is well known, the relative roles of pressure and
viscous stresses, and the rapidly oscillating regime, are less known. Moreover, Stokes (1851)
also considered an outer spherical boundary, deriving implicit bounded-flow solutions and an
explicit force on the sphere. Revisiting these results, we obtained an explicit flow expression
(appendix A.1) and asymptotic expansions of the force for slow and rapid oscillations,
highlighting confinement effects (appendix A.2).
For stress-free boundary conditions, the force differs but remains relevant to the no-slip
configuration, as it directly reflects viscous dissipation in the fluid bulk. This contribution


## Page 8


8
becomes significant when viscosity is not negligible; we therefore recall the corresponding
stress-free bulk-force expressions (appendix A.3).
In the presence of rotation or magnetic effects, the inviscid-fluid force does not necessarily
retain the form 𝑭𝑝= −𝐶𝑖
𝑎𝑚𝑓d𝑡𝑽𝑠; in general 𝑭𝑝is not even opposite to the acceleration
(B74). However, for the three Slichter modes, B74 showed that this property persists when
rotation is included (without magnetic or viscous effects). In this inviscid rotating limit, the
pressure force is parallel to ˆ𝒛for the polar mode and directed along −ˆ𝒔for equatorial modes,
with ˆ𝒔= −ˆ𝒛× (ˆ𝒛× ˆ𝒓) the cylindrical-radius unit vector.

3. Methods: theoretical and numerical approaches
Dimensionless variables use the gap 𝐷= 𝑎𝑚−𝑎𝑠as length scale, the magnetic diffusion
time 𝐷2/𝜂𝑓as time scale, 𝜌𝑓as density scale, and 𝜂𝑓𝐷−1√𝜌𝑓𝜇𝑓as magnetic-field unit.
With the solenoidal constraints ∇· 𝑽= ∇· 𝑩= 0, the governing equations read
𝜕𝑡𝑽+ (𝑽· ∇)𝑽+ 𝛾𝜔ˆ𝒛× 𝑽= −∇𝛱+ 𝑃𝑚∇2𝑽+ (𝑩· ∇)𝑩,
(3.1)
𝜕𝑡𝑩= ˘𝜂∇2𝑩+ (𝑩· ∇)𝑽−(𝑽· ∇)𝑩,
(3.2)
with forcing displacement 𝜹= 𝝐ei(𝜔𝑡+𝑚𝜙) of amplitude 𝜖and frequency 𝜔= 𝜔𝑠𝐷2/𝜂𝑓,
magnetic Prandtl number 𝑃𝑚= 𝜈/𝜂𝑓, and frequency ratio 𝛾= 2𝛺𝑜/𝜔𝑠. The diffusivity is
˘𝜂= 1 in the fluid and ˘𝜂= ˘𝜂𝑠= 𝜂𝑠/𝜂𝑓, ˘𝜂= ˘𝜂𝑚= 𝜂𝑚/𝜂𝑓in the inner and outer solids,
whose velocities are 𝑽= d𝑡𝜹and 𝑽= 0, respectively; only the magnetic equation is solved
in solids. Defining permeability ratios ˘𝜇= ˘𝜇𝑠= 𝜇𝑠/𝜇𝑓and ˘𝜇= ˘𝜇𝑚= 𝜇𝑚/𝜇𝑓, electrically
insulating inner or outer domains correspond to ˘𝜂𝑠→∞and ˘𝜂𝑚→∞.
3.1. Numerical modelling
To validate the theory, equations (2.1)-(2.2) are solved using (a) the finite-difference/pseudo-
spectral code xshells (Schaeffer et al. 2017), or (b) the commercial finite-element code
comsol Multiphysics® when needed. xshells employs a poloidal-toroidal decomposition
of the velocity field in spherical harmonics with degree 𝑙⩽𝑙max and azimuthal wavenumber


## Page 9


9
𝑚⩽𝑚max via the shtns library (Schaeffer 2013), combined with second-order finite
differences on 𝑁𝑟radial points. Its spectral formulation enables access to extreme viscosity
and magnetic-field regimes and has been extensively validated (e.g. Marti et al. 2014). In the
weak-forcing limit 𝜖≪1, the nonlinear equations are integrated in a fixed geometry, with
translational forcing imposed through the boundary velocity d𝑡𝜹at the inner boundary (as in
Rieutord 2002). Implementation details are given in Personnettaz et al. (2026). Simulations
use a semi-implicit third-order backward-difference scheme (SBDF3, Ascher et al. 1995).
A typical resolution at 𝐿𝜈= 10−3, Λ = 0.5, and 𝑃𝑚= 10−2 is 𝑁𝑟= 511, 𝑙max = 127,
and 𝑚max = 8, with sufficient refinement in the nested boundary layers and, when Alfv´en
waves are present, in the interior. For laminar flows with simple forcing (e.g. axial magnetic
field), only a few azimuthal modes are required: polar mode is axisymmetric (𝑚= 0),
whereas equatorial modes require a single additional degree-one component. Simulations
with xshells are restricted here to no-slip boundaries. Dissipation is computed from volume
integrals (§2.1) and time-averaged in the permanent regime. The vorticity formulation avoids
pressure computation, reducing cost but precluding pressure-force evaluation and thus the
added-mass coefficient; in such cases, comsol is used (details in §S1).
3.2. Perturbation approach and boundary-layer theory (BLT)
We consider the weak-forcing regime 𝜖= 𝜖𝑠/𝐷≪1, taken as the smallest length scale.
Analytical solutions are sought using boundary layer theory (BLT). Adopting the time and
azimuthal dependencies ei(𝜔𝑡+𝑚𝜙), polar and equatorial modes correspond to 𝑚= 0 and
𝑚= ±1, respectively. Noting 𝜔= 𝜔𝑠𝜂𝑓/𝐷2, the perturbations are expressed as (figure 3)

in simulations. For 𝑟⩽𝑎𝑠, the field remains uniform when a uniform external field 𝑩𝑒is
applied; its magnitude can reach up to three times 𝑩𝑒for large 𝜇𝑠, or be strongly reduced for
large 𝜇𝑓due to magnetic shielding (see the exact calculation of 𝑩0 in §S2 in this case).
Here, 𝑼and 𝑃denote the hydrodynamic base flow and pressure for 𝑩0 = 0. A non-
zero 𝑩0 and viscous no-slip conditions generate a hierarchy of BLT corrections from inner
and outer boundaries, the latter denoted by (𝑜). These perturbative corrections rely on the
thin boundary-layer assumption, introducing an additional small parameter alongside 𝜖.
Beyond the irrotational solution (𝑼, 𝑃), we compute these BLT contributions incrementally.
We first consider the leading-order corrections (𝒃, 𝒖, 𝑝) and (𝒃(𝑜), 𝒖(𝑜), 𝑝(𝑜)) to the basic

driven by induction and vanishing in the non-magnetic limit where (𝑝) stands for particular,
and a boundary-layer correction (𝒃(ℎ), 𝒖(ℎ)) enforcing the boundary conditions at 𝑟= 𝑎𝑠,
where (ℎ) stands for homogeneous. These corrections in turn force higher-order bulk fields
(B, U, P) and (B(𝑜), U (𝑜), P (𝑜)), smaller by a factor of the boundary-layer thickness.
Boundary conditions finally require additional boundary-layer corrections (𝖇, 𝖚, 𝔭) and
(𝖇(𝑜), 𝖚(𝑜), 𝔭(𝑜)) to (B, U) and (B(𝑜), U (𝑜)), respectively. We outline this hierarchy in

3.4. Higher-order MHD solutions
Equations (3.6)-(3.7) give the boundary-layer solution but do not enforce ∇· 𝒖= 0. To do
so, the radial flow 𝜖Re(𝔲𝑟ei(𝜔𝑡+𝑚𝜙)) is needed at the next order (in the small boundary layer
thickness). With the non-penetration condition, the BLT approach gives (Greenspan 1968)
𝑟𝜕𝑟𝔲𝑟= −cot(𝜃)𝑢𝜃−i𝑚(sin 𝜃)−1𝑢𝜙−𝜕𝜃𝑢𝜃,
(3.13)
where the factor 𝑟on the left-hand side of equation (3.13) is set constant to 𝑟= 𝑎𝑠or
𝑟= 𝑎𝑚. The solution 𝔲𝑟is then obtained by integrating equation (3.13) with the condition
𝔲𝑟(𝑟→∞) = 0, and is non-zero at the boundary (𝔲𝑟being called secondary flow, or Ekman
pumping in rotating fluids). The non-penetration boundary condition 𝔲𝑟+U𝑟= 0 then forces
a secondary bulk flow U (the so-called Ekman circulation for rotating fluids). As diffusive
effects are negligible in the bulk, U and the pressure field P are governed by
i𝜔U = −∇P + 𝑩0 · ∇B + B · ∇𝑩0 −𝛾𝜔ˆ𝒛× U,
(3.14)

with the secondary bulk field B. In contrast to the exponentially decaying 𝔲𝑟, U is nearly
uniform across the boundary layer, with U𝑟(𝑟= 𝑎𝑠) = −𝔲𝑟(𝑟= 𝑎𝑠), so that the radial flow
is 𝔲𝑟+ U𝑟≈𝔲𝑟−𝔲𝑟(𝑟= 𝑎𝑠) in the boundary layer. This higher-order contribution can be
significant (e.g. for recovering the Stokes force; appendix C). Although explicit determination
of (U, B) is difficult, their contribution to mean dissipation can be obtained indirectly via

and rotational effects in (3.14)-(3.15) and assuming an irrotational form; weak rotational
corrections can nevertheless be derived a posteriori for the polar mode (§4.1).
3.5. Boundary-layer approach: forces, detuning and dissipation
We consider the forces on the inner solid domain at leading-order corrections of the basic
state; the ones on the outer solid domain being obtained similarly with the relevant fields at

Ohmic dissipation within the solid side of the boundary must also be evaluated and included

cumbersome and, when required, performed numerically. Analytical simplifications follow
from the BG95 assumptions: (i) retaining only the homogeneous solution, valid for sufficiently
thin boundary layers, and (ii) neglecting spatial variations of boundary-layer thickness, i.e.
assuming a uniform layer. The latter is exact in specific limits (e.g. no rotation with a
monopole field at 𝑟= 𝑎𝑠), and otherwise relies on a characteristic thickness. This approach
is hereafter termed huBL (homogeneous uniform Boundary Layer).
4. Non-magnetic basic flows
We derive the hydrodynamic base flow, which provides the backbone of the MHD boundary-
layer analysis. Describing first the potential flow approach (§4.1) and its bulk dissipation
(§4.2), we then consider a basic flow with order one rotation effects (§4.3).
4.1. Potential flows, added mass and weak rotational perturbations
For rapid oscillations at 𝛾≪1, rotation is negligible. For 𝑩0 = 0, an irrotational bulk
flow 𝑼can then be searched (with finite viscosity, see Buresti 2009), so that 𝑼= ∇𝛷with
∇2𝛷= 0. In the unbounded limit 𝑎𝑠≪𝑎𝑚used by BG95 (or problem 92 in Lamb 1924),
the potential 𝛷can be obtained for arbitrary displacements at small velocities. In the frame
of the sphere, the flow reduces to dipolar contributions with internal sources of the form
(𝑘1𝑥+ 𝑘2𝑦+ 𝑘3𝑧)𝑟−3, where the coefficients 𝑘𝑖are determined from the non-penetration
condition (𝑈𝑟−d𝑡𝜹· ˆ𝒓)|𝑟=𝑎𝑠= 0. Extension to bounded domains follows by superposing
dipolar terms with external sources, as in Art. 9 of Stokes (1843) or problem 93 of Lamb
(1924). In the frame of the outer boundary at rest, additional terms 𝑘4𝑥+ 𝑘5𝑦+ 𝑘6𝑧enforce
non-penetration at 𝑟= 𝑎𝑚. For 𝜖≪1, the flow 𝜖𝑼ei(𝜔𝑡+𝑚𝜙) = 𝜖∇[𝛷ei(𝜔𝑡+𝑚𝜙)] is given by

This bulk-flow dissipation is further enhanced by viscous boundary layers imposed by the

Comparing (4.8) with the 𝐿𝜈-independent term of (A 17) in the unbounded limit 𝑎= 0
further shows that the viscous layer at the no-slip inner boundary reduces the contribution of
this order to the force by a factor 1/2.
4.3. Large rotation effects: basic flow and added mass coefficient
The regime of arbitrary 𝛾is analytically challenging, notably because inertial modes may
be directly forced when 𝛾> 1. In the planetary-relevant range 𝛾< 1, the polar mode was
analysed by B74, while all three modes were considered by Smylie & McMillan (1998, 2000).
These studies include confinement by the outer boundary, and Smylie & McMillan (1998,
2000) additionally account for the associated Stokes-Ekman layers. Their results relevant to
this work are summarised in appendix A.4. We outline below the formulation of B74 used to
analyse rotational effects. Because their expressions contain typographical errors and implicit
relations, we re-derived them, corrected inconsistencies, and obtained explicit forms.

the basic flow becomes comparable to the viscously driven secondary bulk flow U. The
B74 solution again shows the best agreement with the numerical results, whereas alternative
models, including (4.5) (red) and (4.15) (blue), overestimate the velocity, particularly near
the inner boundary. Accurate representation of the bulk flow near 𝑟= 𝑎𝑠is thus essential, as
it sets the boundary-layer solution where dissipation is concentrated.
The meridional fields in figure 6(b), in particular the comparison between 𝑼1 and the
numerical solution (N), further evidence the strong rotational constraint on the bulk flow.
This behaviour is captured only by the B74 solution 𝑼B74, whereas its weakly bounded
Taylor expansion 𝑼2 (𝑎≪1), given by (4.15), fails. Even in approximate form, accounting
for confinement in B74 is therefore essential. Additional meridional cuts from numerical and
analytical models at 𝛾≈1 are provided in Supplementary Material S3. To our knowledge, this
is the first validation and application of the B74 theoretical flow and added-mass coefficient.
5. Induced radial field and inviscid MHD boundary layers
We introduce magnetic effects and derive the leading-order MHD boundary-layer corrections.
We first derive the radial (§5.1) and tangential (§5.2) perturbations, and then infer the bulk
corrections (§5.3), magnetic tension (§5.4), and Ohmic dissipation (§5.5).

These inviscid MHD boundary-layer solutions are shown as dark red dash-dotted lines in
the following figures (e.g. figures 8 and 9 for velocity and magnetic fields). The numerical
velocity perturbation is computed as normalised difference with respect the potential base
flow, Δ𝑽= 𝑽𝜖−1 −Re(𝑼1 exp (i(𝜔𝑡+ 𝑚𝜙))). Without rotation, polar forcing produces
vanishing azimuthal components in both theory and simulations, so only the 𝜃component is
considered. The inviscid MHD solution (dark red dashed) closely reproduces the numerical
magnetic field (orange dotted) in both skin and Alfv´en-wave regimes (figures 9 at Λ𝑙= 2
and 14 at Λ𝑙= 0.5), except near interfaces where viscosity dominates within nested boundary
layers (see insets), leading to slope changes absent from the inviscid model. In the Alfv´en-
wave regime, perturbations penetrate much deeper (right insets showing the full liquid core)
and exhibit wave-like structures (figure 9) compared with the skin-layer regime. In all cases,
the diffusionless induced field (dash-dotted light blue) provides an efficient substitute for the
inviscid MHD particular solution.
Larger discrepancies arise for velocity, especially near solid boundaries (figure 8), where
viscous effects dominate. For large Λ𝑙, the velocity perturbation follows the inviscid MHD
solution outside viscous layers, notably in the Alfv´en-wave regime, whereas viscous control
leads to marked deviations for small Λ𝑙(figure S4). Profiles are shown at co-latitude 𝜃= 30◦;

with 𝜔𝑚𝑡from (5.32); the 𝑎4 term arises solely from P (𝑜). The total magnetic force is
thus ∼3/2 larger the magnetic tension (for moderate 𝑎). Although less accurate than the
dissipated-power estimate, (5.38) indicates comparable real and imaginary detuning, yielding
the magnetic correction to 𝐶𝑖
𝑎, which is inaccessible from the power-based approach.
In Personnettaz et al. (2026), simulations show that the Ohmic dissipation follows the
functional dependence of (5.34) up to an order-unity prefactor. The study focused on the
geophysically relevant range 𝑃𝑚= 10−6-10−3, where viscous corrections are negligible. The
simulations further confirmed the scaling of the Ohmic dissipation as (1 −𝑎3)−2.
6. Magnetohydrodynamic boundary layers with viscous and rotation effects
Viscosity modifies the boundary layers and the pressure force, giving the complete drag. The
study of §5 is extended to viscous fluids, deriving the viscomagnetic oscillatory boundary
layers (§6.1), the associated drag (6.2), and including rotation effects (§6.3).

The tangential boundary-layer solution with viscous and magnetic effects (oscillating
viscomagnetic solution) is shown by thick dark-green curves in radial profiles images (e.g.
figures 13 and 14). Viscosity dominates within one viscous skin depth 𝐿𝜈from the solid
boundaries, where the solution matches both numerical results (orange dotted) and the
purely viscous Stokes layer (dashed violet; §C), as illustrated in the left inset of figure 13.
This agreement persists even under strong magnetic forcing (figure 9, Λ𝑙= 2). Further in
the interior, for 1 < 𝐿𝜈< 2𝐿𝜂, the purely viscous solution matches the numerical velocity
only for very weak local magnetic fields (figure S4, Λ𝑙= 5 × 10−3), whereas the oscillating
viscomagnetic solution remains accurate for any magnetic-field strength (without rotation),
including the Alfv´en-wave regime (figures 8 and 9). In the small-𝑃𝑚limit, the magnetic
field increases the penetration depth of the velocity boundary layer. For the polar mode, the

Rotation does not appear in (6.12) because the leading 𝛾-correction is purely azimuthal;
however, viscously induced pressure corrections reintroduce rotational effects in the total
force 𝑭𝜈. As shown in appendix A.4, the results of Smylie & McMillan (1998, 2000) are
recovered from (6.14) at order 𝛾, with higher-order corrections modifying Δ𝜔𝑣. Appendix
C further recovers the Stokes result and the detuning from a no-slip outer boundary by
multiplying (6.12) by 𝑎. Pressure contributions follow from the bulk-flow force 𝑭𝑝associated
with U, yielding Re(Δ𝜔𝜈) and Im(Δ𝜔𝜈). When only dissipation is required, Im(Δ𝜔𝜈) can
instead be obtained directly from (3.16). Both approaches are detailed in appendix C, where
the leading-order Stokes limits (A 16)-(A 17) are recovered and extended to weak rotation.

(6.15) also follows from the O(𝛾4) expansion (4.15) of the B74 bulk flow 𝑼(appendix A.5).
Equations (6.14)-(6.15) show that the total viscous dissipation exceeds the tangential-stress
contribution by 3(1 + 𝛾2/5)/[2(1 −𝑎3)]; neglecting hydrodynamic or magnetic pressure
therefore omits rotation and geometric effects, modifying the scaling for 𝑎∼1. For a no-slip
outer boundary, the corresponding force and detuning are obtained by multiplying (6.15) by
𝑎4. The total dissipative force is thus (1+𝑎4) times (6.15), recovering the leading-order term
of (A 17) in the rapid-oscillation limit 𝐿𝜈→0 (appendix C).
Discarding magnetic and rotation effects, one can also extend the results to equatorial
modes. Re-introducing the 𝜙-dependency factor ei𝜙, and using the Cartesian basis vectors
to ease the integral calculation, we obtain that the total force 𝑭𝑣, in each of the ˆ𝒙and ˆ𝒚
directions (or the d𝑡𝜹and d2
𝑡𝜹directions), is equal to the force in the ˆ𝒛direction for the polar
mode. All the previous results, notably related to Δ𝜔𝑣, hold thus for the equatorial mode. This
agrees with Smylie & McMillan (2000), who obtained the same force for the polar and the
equatorial modes in the limit of vanishing rotation effects and outer boundary displacement.

6.3. Magnetohydrodynamic Stokes–Ekman boundary layer
Extension to rotating viscous flows with finite 𝛾is achieved by reintroducing the Coriolis
term, yielding MHD Stokes-Ekman boundary layers. As before, the general boundary-layer
solution is determined in terms of a given basic flow 𝑼and particular solution. Integration
of the homogeneous part of (3.6)-(3.7) then gives, at both boundaries,

## Page 39


39
For instance, the four boundary layers (6.19)-(6.20) are given by Gans (1970, 1971). These
layers have also been examined near electrically conducting solids for MHD inertial waves
(Kerswell 1994) and precession-nutation flows (Mathews & Guo 2005; Deleplace & Cardin
2006). Asymptotic limits are reviewed in §S4 of the Supplementary Material.
The prefactors (𝐴𝑗, 𝐵𝑗) in (6.17)-(6.18) follow from the boundary conditions. Discarding
the particular solution isolates the contribution independent of it (superscript (0), as in 5.12).

where 𝜔sin 𝜃arises from boundary motion (absent at 𝑟= 𝑎𝑚), and 𝑋= ˇ𝑋(1 + i)/𝐿𝜂for
ˇ𝜄± and ˇ𝜅±. The 𝜙components vanish in the non-rotating limit. Further progress requires
specifying both 𝑼and the particular solution. For the polar mode, 𝑼follows from (4.9), but
its complexity precludes an analytical particular solution; a diffusionless approximation is
nevertheless obtained for weak Lorentz forces (appendix B.1).
With rotation, polar forcing generates finite azimuthal velocity and magnetic components
(figure 16). For weak rotation (e.g. 𝛾= 0.1), numerical results are well reproduced by
the MHD Stokes-Ekman model associated with the rotation-corrected potential base flow
(4.5) (red dotted). Rotation also deepens the penetration of boundary-layer perturbations
relative to the purely viscous skin layer (violet dashed) and the viscomagnetic oscillatory
solution (green), as seen near the inner boundary in the left insets of figure 16. Adding the
diffusionless particular solution to the homogeneous MHD Stokes-Ekman model (solid red)
yields an accurate prediction of the azimuthal magnetic perturbation (figure 16b). At 𝛾= 0.1,
the equatorial modes are accurately captured by the viscomagnetic oscillatory model, which
reproduces both polar and azimuthal velocity and magnetic perturbations (green lines in S9).
At larger 𝛾, the model based on the base flow (4.3) fails to reproduce the numerical results
for equatorial modes, see figure S10 for 𝛾= 0.33.
For the polar mode, the Stokes-Ekman solution based on the rotation-corrected potential
base flow (4.5) loses accuracy at larger 𝛾, e.g. 𝛾= 0.92, as shown by the red curves in figure
17. The discrepancy between the rotation-corrected potential base flow and B74 (dark blue
line) is of the same order as the boundary-layer perturbation. Using 𝑼B74 in the MHD Stokes-
Ekman solution (gray in figure 17) restores accurate estimates of both polar and azimuthal
velocity and magnetic perturbations, notably near 𝑟= 𝑎𝑠.

7. Conclusion
7.1. Summary
Despite its simple forcing, translational oscillation has received only fragmented attention,
without a unified treatment combining the various effects. We thus provided a structured
synthesis of the main contributions, clarifying a previously disjoint literature. Our work
connects four key studies related to an oscillating sphere in a cavity: viscous hydrodynamic
solution (Stokes 1851); the inviscid rotating solution (Busse 1974); the MHD boundary-
layer theory in an unbounded inviscid fluid (Buffett & Goertz 1995) and the viscous rotating
solution of Smylie & McMillan (1998, 2000) that discard the viscosity-induced pressure
effects. The first three address the polar mode, whereas the last also considers equatorial
modes; their respective limitations are summarised in the first four lines of table 2. All rely
on non-trivial analytical derivations, but none had been validated against simulations in a
fluid-filled shell.
We have reassessed these studies using MHD simulations and extended them to include
confinement, electromagnetic contrasts between solid domains, equatorial modes, and rota-
tional corrections to the polar mode. The resulting fluid response yields forces decomposed
into in-phase and out-of-phase components, given by the real and imaginary parts of the
detuning vector 𝚫𝝎. The in-phase contribution defines the added-mass coefficient, whereas
viscous and Ohmic effects appear in the out-of-phase component, equivalently measured

expressions and their validity ranges.
The unified boundary-layer framework shows that MHD drag arises from three coupled
mechanisms: Alfv´en-wave radiation into the fluid bulk, viscous stresses within thin shear
layers, and Ohmic dissipation associated with electromagnetic coupling across interfaces.
Confinement and electromagnetic contrasts modify both amplitude and phase of the MHD
loading, while rotation reorganises the response through MHD Stokes-Ekman boundary
layers and, for the polar mode, a rotation-corrected flow derived from Busse (1974). The
theory captures both axisymmetric polar oscillations and non-axisymmetric equatorial modes
within a single asymptotic description.
These results provide a quantitative framework for oscillatory flows in confined rotating
MHD systems. In planetary settings, they quantify how translational oscillations of solid
regions dissipate energy through coupled viscous and electromagnetic processes, with
relevance to Earth’s Slichter modes and to strongly confined subsurface oceans of icy moons.
More broadly, the present formulation establishes a canonical benchmark for oscillatory
MHD coupling in spherical geometry, amenable to laboratory realisation and numerical
validation, and providing a consistent basis for incorporating rotation, magnetic fields and
confinement in future studies of planetary interior dynamics.
7.2. Perspective
A first limitation concerns equatorial modes at non-negligible rotation, for which no analytical
base flow is available. Further developments are therefore required to obtain a tractable
description of rotation-modified equatorial modes. A promising route is to build upon the
rotation-corrected basic flow of Smylie & McMillan (1998), allowing consistent evaluation
of detuning including rotational, magnetic and viscous effects. Their formulation, close to an
inertial-mode basis, could enable exploration of strong-rotation regimes (𝛾> 1), although
inviscid inertial modes in a spherical shell are singular and only regularised by viscosity.
Another limitation stems from the small-amplitude assumption used in both the theory and
the approximate boundary condition implemented in xshells, which requires amplitudes
smaller than the boundary layer. While appropriate for planets, this is limiting in laboratory
configurations. As noted in Personnettaz et al. (2026), the approximate boundary condi-
tion overestimates dissipation at larger amplitudes. Using comsol simulations, where the
Arbitrary-Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE) method allows a truly moving inner sphere, we have


## Page 43


43
checked that the agreement remains good for modest deformations. Further work is needed,
particularly regarding Ohmic dissipation and the transition to finite-amplitude regimes.
Experimental validation is both feasible and desirable. Table 3 compares the typical
ranges relevant for planetary applications to the ones that can are typically achieved in
laboratory experiments. Liquid-metal experiments readily access regimes where Alfv´en-
wave radiation becomes significant (Λ ≳1), for instance in a 50 cm Galinstan-filled cavity
with an oscillating iron or titanium sphere. Moderate to large electromagnetic contrasts arise
then naturally in laboratory configurations. Our theoretical framework also accommodates
differing electromagnetic properties in the surrounding outer domain, providing additional
experimental flexibility.
Further extensions include non-spherical inner bodies, beginning with spheroids using the
potential-flow of Lai & Mockros (1972), and boundary layers along spheroidal interfaces
(e.g. C´ebron et al. 2021). Incorporating density stratification and gravity-wave coupling
(Voisin 2024) would also be relevant for planetary interiors such as Mercury’s stratified
core (Seuren et al. 2023). Finally, the present model treats Alfv´en-wave radiation without
wave-wave interactions or reflections (Schaeffer & Jault 2016) ; including these effects would
provide a more complete description of wave-mediated dissipation.
The theory also predicts zero torques, whereas simulations exhibit finite mean torques
in the presence of rotation effects, indicating higher-order effects in oscillation amplitude.
Rapid oscillations generate steady streaming at order O(𝜖2), producing mean zonal flows
and modified dissipation (e.g. C´ebron et al. 2021, for rapid rotation regimes). Extending this
study to include such nonlinear corrections would clarify the role of oscillations and rotation
induced mean secondary flows.

Appendix A. Revisiting previous non-magnetic studies
A.1. Explicit expression of the confined solution of Stokes (1851)
Equations 54-57 of Stokes (1851) can be analytically integrated to obtain the exact flow
solution of the non-rotating problem. This exact viscous solution is continuously valid from
the thin-gap limit 𝑎𝑚−𝑎𝑠≪𝐿𝜈to the opposite large gap limit, as well as from the steady
limit 𝜔→0 of large 𝐿𝜈≫𝑎𝑚> 𝑎𝑠to the rapidly oscillating limit of small 𝐿𝜈≪𝑎𝑠< 𝑎𝑚.

figure 20). For the friction term Im(𝐾), this regime implies linear scaling with viscosity,

a minimum 𝐶𝑎≈1.46 at 𝑎= 𝑎𝑚𝑖𝑛(figure 20). Solving 𝜕𝑎𝐶𝑎= 0 from (A 18) yields a
cumbersome exact expression, but a second-order Taylor expansion about 𝑎≈1/2 provides
the accurate estimate 𝑎𝑚𝑖𝑛= 87133/169336 ≈0.515 (relative error < 10−4).
A.3. Viscous force on oscillating stress-free spheres
For a stress-free sphere, 𝑭𝑣= 0 and 𝑭𝑛reads (Yang & Leal 1991; Zhang et al. 2023)
𝑭𝑛= −𝐶𝑎𝑚𝑓d𝑡𝑽𝑠−𝑎𝑠𝜌𝑓𝜈(4𝜋𝑽𝑠+ 𝑭𝑖).
(A 20)
where 𝐶𝑎= 1/2. The viscous part of 𝑭𝑛, contains a non-Basset memory integral contribution
𝑭𝑖, that vanishes for no-slip spheres and that is generally not in phase with 𝑽𝑠(Yang &
Leal 1991). Equation (2.13) of Zhang et al. (2023) provides a general expression for 𝑭𝑖,
which shows that 𝑭𝑖simplifies into 8𝜋𝑽and 0 in the respective limits of rapid and slow
oscillations. The respective viscous force reduces then to the Levich drag force with the
numerical prefactor 12𝜋(Levich 1949; Joseph & Wang 2004), and to the Stokes (Hadamard-
Rybzynski) drag force with the numerical prefactor 4𝜋(Yang & Leal 1991). In both cases,
2/3 comes from normal viscous stress and 1/3 from the viscously modified pressure field.
A.4. Revisiting the results of Smylie & McMillan (1998, 2000)
Smylie & McMillan (1998, 2000) compute the flows associated with polar and equatorial
translational modes, neglecting magnetic effects but retaining rotation for 𝛾< 1. Although
inertial modes are not excited in this regime, they seek solutions of analogous structure
(products of Legendre functions) and derive the flow driven by oscillations of a spheroidal
inner boundary. While the rotation-perturbed flow (Eq. 4.5) is restricted to leading order
in 𝛾, their formulation includes higher-order corrections. To simplify the enforcement of
boundary conditions, they neglect the small eccentricity of the inner boundary, but account
for a mobile outer spherical boundary. The fluid is thus enclosed within a solid shell (of
mass 𝑀𝑠in their notation) whose motion ensures conservation of total linear momentum
across the three layers. Viscous effects at the inner boundary are incorporated through an
oscillatory Stokes-Ekman layer, assuming 𝐿𝜈≪𝑎𝑠. However, the viscous layer at the outer

Appendix B. Magnetohydrodynamic particular solution driven by bulk flows
The particular solution forced by the bulk flow has not been obtained analytically in the
general case. Yet, it could be estimated in certain limits. Below, we consider first the limit of
diffusionless magnetic fields, and then the limit of large magnetic diffusivities.

The diffusionless solution accurately captures the perturbation field outside boundary
layers, as illustrated by the dot-dashed blue curve in figures 7, and also provides an effective
approximate particular solution within the boundary-layer problem. As demonstrated in
several places (e.g. figure 9), it closely reproduces the inviscid MHD particular solution. From
a computational standpoint, this approximation is advantageous as it avoids exponential and
hyperbolic sine integral functions. This efficiency is particularly valuable when the analytical
particular solution is unavailable, as in the case of the Stokes-Ekman boundary layer or when
we use the base flow of B74. In figure 16b, adding the diffusionless magnetic field solution to
homogeneous Stokes-Ekman boundary layer allows to perfectly match the numerical result.

In Personnettaz et al. (2026), Ohmic dissipation in this ’diffusive’ regime was computed
with simulations, showing excellent agreement with the above analytical expressions for both
polar and equatorial modes, and confirming the dependence on the confinement parameter 𝑎.
Although not relevant for molten-metal cores, this regime may apply to shallow subsurface
oceans (figure 1). The large-diffusivity limit is also pertinent to laboratory experiments and
has been studied previously for polar forcing. For example, Singh (1965) derived the drag
on a conducting sphere oscillating along a uniform magnetic field in a compressible viscous
unbounded fluid. For a dielectric sphere in a non-rotating inviscid fluid, Motz (1966b)
extended the theory to strong magnetic fields and validated it experimentally.
Appendix C. Non-magnetic Stokes boundary-layer solution
For rapid oscillations of a solid sphere in a viscous fluid, the fluid force is dominated by the
flow in the thin boundary layer and the boundary layer theory (BLT) can then be used instead
of the exact lengthy calculation of Stokes (1851) discussed in appendix A.1.


## Page 52


52

SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL
S1. COMSOL simulation details
In the comsol finite element model (b), we use the Arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian approach
(ALE) to translate the inner sphere and modify the mesh in accordance with its imposed
kinematics. This method allows for arbitrary displacements, and enables stress-free or no-
slip boundary conditions at the inner or outer boundaries. Considering the magnetic field
departure 𝑩1 from an imposed magnetic field 𝑩0, equation (3.2) for 𝑩= 𝑩0 + 𝑩1 shows that
𝑩1 = ∇× 𝑨can be obtained from the equation
𝜕𝑡𝑨= 𝑽× (𝑩0 + 𝑩1) + ˘𝜂∇2𝑨,
(S1 1)
with the magnetic potential vector 𝑨. The non-linear MHD solution is then obtained by
integrating equations (3.1) and (S1 1) in the fluid and in the two solid domains using their
respective magnetic diffusivity ˘𝜂(C´ebron et al. 2012a). To consider an unbounded outer
solid domain, a typical radius > 7𝑎𝑠is used. Because of the gauge used by comsol, all
domains should be electrically conducting and we typically use a conductivity 103 smaller
than the fluid one to approximate insulating domains.
While equatorial modes require three-dimensional meshes, the polar mode can be sim-
ulated using on the 2D meridional plane of an axisymmetric model. The finite element
method coupled with the ALE is computationally intensive and, hence, will be limited to
large viscosity. Nevertheless, it allowed one to examine limitations of parametric boundary
conditions. Furthermore, we can compute the forces at both boundaries, as the pressure field
is a by-product of the numerical solution. To compute the added mass coefficient 𝐶𝑎, we
record the values of the acceleration part of the total force 𝐹𝑛+ 𝐹𝑣exerted at the boundary,
when the velocity of the inner sphere is null (see e.g. figure 19, where the yellow segment
is proportional to the added mass coefficient). Both xshells and comsol can compute all
magnetic forces and dissipation magnitude, including magnetic corrections of the added
mass coefficient. However, only comsol can determine the inviscid added mass coefficient
or its viscous correction (as they require the pressure, not computed in xshells).
S2. Relating the basic magnetic field to an externally imposed one
Although the boundary-layer theory developed in this work is valid for an arbitrary 𝜙-
independent 𝑩0, it is useful to relate this field to an externally imposed field 𝑩𝑒through the
magnetic permeabilities of the different domains. This relation can be obtained analytically
for a uniform 𝑩𝑒and a spherical outer solid medium of permeability 𝜇𝑚and outer radius 𝑎𝑒.
To this end, dipolar contributions are added to 𝑩𝑒in each domain, arising from internal and
external sources of respective form (𝑘1𝑥+ 𝑘2𝑦+ 𝑘3𝑧)𝑟−3 and 𝑘4𝑥+ 𝑘5𝑦+ 𝑘6𝑧. Assuming,

𝑯= ∇(𝑘𝑠𝑧) to ensure regularity at the centre. In the two other domains, the field reads
𝑯= ∇(𝑘𝑖𝑧𝑟−3 + 𝑘𝑗𝑧). The 6 coefficients 𝑘𝑖are then determined by imposing continuity of

S3. Polar mode: confinement and strong rotation effects on the bulk flow
In figure S1, meridional plots of the three velocity components forced by polar oscillation are
showed (a) 𝛾= 0.92 and (b) 𝛾= 0.99. There, we compare B74 solution (𝑼B74), the rotation
perturbed potential flow 𝑼1 (4.5), and its correction 𝑼2 (4.15) with numerical (N) simulation
(meridional plane, zoom up to 𝑟= 6𝑎𝑠). The B74 solution (𝑼B74) effectively captures the
influence of rotation. Rotation significantly modifies the potential flow 𝑼1, which classically
propagates in the axial direction. This modification results in a velocity distribution that
spreads outward in the equatorial region.
S4. Magnetohydrodynamic boundary layers
S4.1. Polar mode and uniform axial field: magnetic tension vs. magnetic pressure
One can wonder how the magnetic tension compares with the magnetic pressure force from
𝒃. Considering the polar mode, the z-component of the magnetic force should then be
calculated. While the BLT gives 𝒇𝑚𝑡· ˆ𝒛≈𝐵0𝑟𝒃∥· ˆ𝒛= −𝐵0𝑏𝜃sin 𝜃cos 𝜃, the magnetic
pressure contribution is 𝒇𝑚𝑝· ˆ𝒛= 𝑓𝑚𝑝ˆ𝒓· ˆ𝒛= 𝐵0𝑏𝜃sin 𝜃cos 𝜃since 𝑓𝑚𝑝= −𝑩0 · 𝒃. For
our uniform axial field, the magnetic tension is thus cancelled out by the magnetic pressure
force. This can actually be recovered from the Lorentz force, given by (∇× 𝒃) × 𝑩0 at this
order, which is perpendicular to 𝑩0, and thus to the 𝑧-axis for an axial field. The Ohmic
dissipation (5.34)-(5.35) is thus transferred to the solid inner domain through the pressure

In the quasi-steady limit 𝜔→0, the solution at 𝜃= 0 reduces to the plane Ekman-
Hartmann layer familiar from spin-up studies. In particular, Benton & Loper (1969) derived
two complex wavenumbers 𝑘and 𝑚(their equations 30-36). Comparing their steady solution
(equations 51-52) with the steady limit of (6.19)-(6.20) shows that their 𝑚= 0 corresponds to
𝜄+ = −𝜅−= 0, while their 𝑘is recovered from 𝜅+ = 𝜄∗
−(with ∗denoting complex conjugation).
In their scaling, 𝑘= 𝜅+𝐿𝛺= [Λ𝛺+ (Λ2
𝛺)1/2]1/2, where 𝐿𝛺= 𝐿𝜂(𝑃𝑚/𝛾)1/2 is the Ekman
depth and Λ𝛺= Λ𝑙/𝛾+ i cos 𝜃, reducing to Λ𝛺= Λ𝑧/𝛾+ i at 𝜃= 0.
For a uniform magnetic field bounded by an infinite rigid insulating plate, Debnath (1974)
analysed oscillatory plane boundary layers (recovered here at 𝜃= 0) including viscous,
rotational, and magnetic effects. However, the Lorentz force was approximated as (∇× 𝑩) ×
𝑩= −𝜎𝑓𝜌−1
𝑓𝐵2
0𝑽(see equation 2.5 of Debnath 1972), which assumes (i) negligible electric
and induced magnetic fields and (ii) velocity predominantly perpendicular to 𝑩0 (Rossow
1958). While valid for variants of Hartmann layers (see also equation 10.16 of Jackson
1977) or certain quasi-static MHD regimes (Thess & Zikanov 2007), this approximation
suppresses magnetic perturbations and yields only two wavenumbers, in contrast with the


## Page 64


6
four in (6.19)-(6.20). Consequently, the radial wavenumber 𝜆of BG95 cannot be recovered
as a limiting case. The magnetic Stokes-Ekman layer considered here therefore generalises
the Stokes-Ekman-Hartmann layer of Debnath (1974).
S5. Theory–simulation comparison
In this section, we propose further comparison of the boundary layer models with numerical
results to cross-validate the theoretical development and the simulations.
S5.1. Boundary layers at small Λ𝑙
In the presence of a weak Λ𝑙, i.e. a weak magnetic field, the velocity boundary layer follows
the viscous skin layer solution (derived in appendix C), in dash dotted violet, in figure S4
(Λ𝑙= 5 · 10−3) for equatorial forcing. The inviscid MHD solutions (red dashed and azure
dash dotted) result in negligible velocities. The viscomagnetic oscillatory solution (green
solid) converge to the purely viscous boundary layer for the velocity. Including the second
order viscous bulk flow, can help to reconcile the numerical results with the theory, as shown
in the right insets of S4b. The numerical radial velocity perturbation, orange dotted in figure
S4a, are perfectly captured by the higher order viscomagnetic oscillatory solution and the
purely viscous boundary layer. The viscomagnetic oscillatory solution (green solid) describes
perfectly the magnetic field perturbations S5. Analogous agreement for both velocity and
magnetic perturbations was found for polar forcing at small Λ𝑙.
S5.2. Boundary layers in the weak-rotation regime
Figures S6 and S7 show for the polar forcing that the MHD Stokes-Ekman boundary-layer
solution based on 𝑼1 closely matches the numerical results. For the radial component, the
secondary bulk flow U𝑟combines with 𝔲𝑟to shape the near-boundary profile. In contrast, for
tangential components, its contribution is significant only in the bulk where the boundary-
layer solution vanishes; there, the MHD Stokes-Ekman secondary flow accurately reproduces
the perturbation, as highlighted in the insets. In this weak-rotation regime, velocity effects are
predominantly azimuthal. The diffusionless solution 𝑏( 𝑝)
0
provides an accurate approximation
of the magnetic particular solution. For the equatorial forcing in the weak rotation regime,
the MHD Stokes-Ekman solution (dotted red) coincides with the simpler MHD oscillatory
solution, as shown in figure S8 and figure S9.
In figure S10, we present the velocity profiles for an equatorial forcing in the presence
of moderate rotation (𝛾= 0.33). By comparing with the numerical result in dotted yellow,
we see that all viscous solutions capture quite well the evolution within a viscous length.
The position of the maximum of the velocity boundary layer profile is well captured by
the MHD Stokes-Ekman layer. The bulk value instead is not well estimated, the reason of
this discrepancy is that we adopt the potential flow solution (4.3), that does not account for
rotation effects.
S6. Role of electromagnetic properties in regime transitions
In figure S11, the dependency of the magnetic tension with respect to ˘𝜂1/2
𝑠
˘𝜇𝑠and Λ𝑙is
shown through the proxy J𝑠Λ−1/2
𝑙
(see equation 5.32). Matching its leading-order asymptotic
expansions at small and large Λ, we obtain a transition between these two asymptotic
behaviours at Λ𝑙= 2( ˘𝜂1/2
𝑠
